1
footnotetext:
This research was supported by NSF Grants DMS-2015285 and DMS-2052955.

1
Introduction

Independent component analysis (ICA) is a powerful and general data analysis tool. It was initially introduced as a blind source separation technique
(
Jutten and Herault 1991
;
Comon 1994
)
and has since found tremendous success in applications from numerous scientific and engineering fields such as neuroimaging, cognitive science, signal processing, and machine learning, just to name a few. The basic premise of ICA is that the coordinates of a random vector
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be expressed as linear combinations of
d
independent latent variables
S_{1},\ldots,S_{d}
such that
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}
where
\mathbf{S}=(S_{1},\ldots,S_{d})^{\top}
and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is a deterministic but unknown matrix, referred to as the mixing matrix. ICA can be viewed as a refinement of the principal component analysis (PCA) which allows us to write each coordinate of
\mathbf{X}
as linear combinations of
d
uncorrelated random variables. While there are infinitely many ways to express
\mathbf{X}
as linear transformations of uncorrelated variables, remarkably, there is essentially only one way to write it as linear transformations of independent random variables if there is at most one Gaussian source among
S_{1},\ldots,S_{d}
, i.e., the mixing matrix
\mathbf{A}
is identifiable up to rescaling and signed permutation of its columns. See, e.g.
Comon 1994
;
Eriksson and Koivunen 2004
.

The goal of ICA is to recover from a sample, consisting of
n
independent copies
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
of
\mathbf{X}
, the independent components
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{S}_{n}=\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{n}
, or equivalently to estimate the mixing matrix
\mathbf{A}
. Over the years, numerous methods and algorithms have been developed for this purpose. Notable examples include
Delfosse and Loubaton 1995
;
Pham 1996
;
Pham and Garat 1997
;
Amari and Cardoso 1997
;
Cardoso 1999
;
Hyvarinen 1999
;
Lee et al. 1999
;
Hastie and Tibshirani 2002
;
Eriksson and Koivunen 2003
,
Samarov and Tsybakov 2004
;
Chen and Bickel 2005
;
Chen and Bickel 2006
;
Ilmonen and Paindaveine 2011
;
Samworth and Yuan 2012
;
Belkin et al. 2018
among numerous others. See, e.g.,
Cardoso and Comon 1996
;
Hyvärinen and Oja 2000
;
Roberts and Everson 2001
;
Stone 2004
;
Comon and Jutten 2010
;
Nordhausen and Oja 2018
for overviews and surveys of a wide-ranging list of algorithms and applications of ICA. Most of these earlier developments for ICA have focused on the classical fixed dimension paradigm when the number of sources is fixed and the sample size diverges. Both the statistical and algorithmic aspects of ICA in such a setting are by now well-understood.

There are, however, an increasing amount of empirical evidence that these theories cannot fully capture the complexity, both statistically and computationally, of the problem or explain the lackluster performance of these methods often observed in modern applications. This predicament can be largely attributed to the effect of dimension
d
which is largely unaccounted for and has inspired a flurry of recent research to better understand its role in ICA. See, e.g.,
Belkin et al. 2013
;
Anandkumar et al. 2014b
;
Anandkumar et al. 2014c
;
Bhaskara et al. 2014
;
Goyal et al. 2014
;
Vempala and Xiao 2015
;
Voss et al. 2015
;
Belkin et al. 2018
among others. These works typically take an algorithmic viewpoint and focus on developing estimation procedures with both sample and computational complexities that are polynomials of
d
. Yet it remains unclear what the best possible sample complexity is, and how considerations of computational tractability may affect it. Even less is known about the statistical properties of ICA when
d
is not assumed fixed. The goal of the current work is to specifically address these fundamental questions, and develop estimating procedures for ICA that are both statistically efficient and computationally tractable.

In particular, we derive information theoretical limits for ICA by establishing the minimax optimal rates for estimating the mixing matrix
\mathbf{A}
. The minimax optimal rates of convergence can be equivalently expressed in terms of the optimal sample complexity: a sample size
n\gtrsim d/\varepsilon^{2}
is necessary and sufficient to ensure the existence of estimates of the unmixing directions with an error up to
\varepsilon
. To prove these bounds, we show that no estimates can converge at faster rates and also introduce an estimate that can attain these rates. The minimax optimal estimation technique is based on two crucial observations. The first is that, if
\mathbf{X}
is centered and pre-whitened so that its covariance matrix is the identity matrix, then the column vectors of
\mathbf{A}
can be identified with linear transforms of
\mathbf{X}
that maximize the kurtosis. Interestingly, we show that while maximizing sample kurtosis leads to consistent estimates of column vectors of the mixing matrix when
n\gg d^{2}
, it is necessarily inconsistent when
n\ll d^{2}
and thus cannot be minimax optimal at least in the regime
d\ll n\ll d^{2}
. To overcome this difficulty, we appeal to another key insight that, for any given
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we can derive estimates of the kurtosis of
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
with improved tail behavior than the sample kurtosis. This builds upon the sub-gaussian mean estimation framework that has recently received significant attention; see, e.g.,
Catoni 2012
;
Minsker 2015
;
Hsu and Sabato 2016
;
Lugosi and Mendelson 2016
;
Minsker 2018
;
Ke et al. 2019
. These estimates of kurtosis can be ensembled to allow for better control of the estimation error of kurtosis uniformly over all linear transformations, and subsequently, lead to minimax optimal estimate of the mixing matrix.

The minimax optimal estimates we introduced however incur a prohibitive computational cost and are not feasible for problems of large or even moderate dimensions. A natural question is whether or not a computationally tractable estimating procedure can be minimax optimal. More specifically, we focus our attention on a large class of computationally tractable algorithms called low-degree polynomial algorithms. See, e.g.,
Hopkins 2018
;
Kunisky et al. 2022
;
Schramm and Wein 2022
. We show that no consistent estimate of the mixing matrix can be computed using such algorithms if
n\lesssim d^{2}
, up to a logarithmic factor, which leads to a lower bound of
n\gtrsim\max\{d^{2},d/\varepsilon^{2}\}
(up to a logarithmic factor) for optimal sample size over this class of algorithms. This sample size requirement is indeed (nearly) optimal as we also develop a computationally tractable estimating procedure that attains this sample complexity.

Our estimate is motivated by a close inspection of the popular FastICA algorithm and its pitfalls when
d
is not small or fixed. FastICA can be viewed as a fixed-point algorithm for maximizing sample kurtosis. Because of the inherent nonconvexity of the underlying optimization problem, it is well known that the performance of FastICA is sensitive to its initialization. See, e.g.,
Zarzoso et al. 2006
. And this is especially problematic in moderate or large dimensions when random initialization can only yield a nontrivial estimate of the mixing direction with exponentially (in
d
) many tries. To address this challenge, we propose a new initialization strategy that is guaranteed to produce a nontrivial initial estimate whenever
n\gtrsim d^{2}
. The proposed initialization procedure is prompted by a careful inspection of how random slicing behaves and is based on improved moment estimators. With such an initialization, we show that the fixed-point iteration and deflation steps of FastICA are in fact effective in high dimensions, yielding an estimate that is both computationally tractable and statistically optimal. In addition to the convergence rates, we also establish the distributional properties of our estimate. Specifically, we provide normal approximations to estimates of linear and bilinear forms of the mixing matrix. These results can be readily used to construct confidence intervals or conduct hypothesis testing. The close connection with the FastICA also means that our estimating procedure can be easily implemented and the practical merits of the proposed method are further demonstrated by numerical, both simulated and real data, examples.

Our work is closely related to a fast-growing literature on using tensor methods in statistics and machine learning. See, e.g.,
Kolda and Bader 2009
;
Anandkumar et al. 2014a
;
Cichocki et al. 2015
;
Sidiropoulos et al. 2017
. In particular, we leverage the fact that unmixing directions can be identified with the eigenvectors of a cumulant tensor and the estimation error of the mixing matrix can therefore be viewed as the perturbation effect of the sample cumulant tensor. There is a fruitful line of research in developing algorithm-dependent bounds for general perturbed low-rank tensors. See, e.g.,
Anandkumar et al. 2014b
;
Mu et al. 2015
;
Mu et al. 2017
;
Belkin et al. 2018
;
Zhang and Xia 2018
;
Auddy and Yuan 2022
;
Auddy and Yuan 2023
. But an observation key to our development is that the estimation error of the sample cumulant tensor has unique structures and appropriately leveraging them can lead to more efficient algorithms and sharper statistical performance. In particular, our results suggest that the (un)mixing directions can be estimated at a much faster rate (i.e.,
\sqrt{d/n}
) than the sample kurtosis tensor (i.e.,
\sqrt{d^{2}/n}
). Also as a byproduct of our analysis, we show that if the signals have nonvanishing excess kurtosis, then there is virtually no benefit in considering higher-order moments in ICA.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In the next section, we study the information theoretical limits of ICA as well as the limits for estimates that can be computed using low-degree polynomial algorithms. Section
3
introduces our proposed ICA method and proves that it is both polynomial-time computable and minimax optimal when
n\gtrsim d^{2}
. Section
4
presents the asymptotic properties of the proposed estimate. To complement the theoretical and methodological developments, numerical experiments are given in Section
5
to demonstrate the practical merits of our approach. We conclude with a brief summary in Section
6
. All proofs are relegated to the Appendix for space considerations.

2
Information Theoretical and Computational Limits

In this section, we establish the information theoretical limits for ICA and develop an approach that achieves them. This is followed up by a study of the impact of computational constraints on the optimal sample complexity.

2.1
Setup and Minimax Lower Bounds

Without loss of generality, we shall assume in the rest of the paper that each component of source
\mathbf{S}
is centered and has a unit variance. In addition, we shall assume that each signal
S_{j}
has finite
(8+\epsilon)
th moment for some
\epsilon>0
, and their (excess) kurtosis
\kappa_{4}(S_{j}):=\mathbb{E}(S_{j}^{4})-3
is bounded away from zero and infinity. Note that all our results can be straightforwardly generalized to the case when one source has zero excess kurtosis, e.g., when it is Gaussian. This is because its corresponding (un)mixing direction can be determined by virtue of all the other directions. For brevity, we shall not delve into such a special case in what follows. Denote by
{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
the collection of all probability measures of
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
that follows an ICA model with mixing matrix
\mathbf{A}
and latent signal
\mathbf{S}
obeying these assumptions, that is,

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})=\{{\cal L}(\mathbf{X}):\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S},\,M_{1}^{-1}\leq|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})|\leq M_{1},\,\mathbb{E}(|S_{j}|^{8+\epsilon})\leq M_{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}S_{j}=0,\,\mathbb{E}S_{j}^{2}=1,\,S_{j}{\rm s\ are\ independent}\},

where
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})
is the law of the random variable
\mathbf{X}
.

It is also customary in the ICA literature to pre-whiten the data so that one can assume that
\texttt{cov}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{I}_{d}
. To see the rationale behind pre-whitening and the simplification it brings about, consider the case when
\texttt{cov}(\mathbf{X})=\Sigma
is known. Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}
. It is clear that
\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}
has an identity covariance matrix and also follows an ICA model with mixing matrix
\mathbf{B}:=\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathbf{A}
. In other words, there is no loss of generality to assume that
\texttt{cov}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{I}_{d}
if the covariance matrix of
\mathbf{X}
is known in advance. Note that both the new mixing matrix
\mathbf{B}
and its corresponding unmixing matrix (
\mathbf{B}^{-1}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}
) belong to the set
{\cal O}(d)
of orthonormal
d\times d
matrices. Note that there is no loss of generality to adopt this simplifying assumption when discussing information theoretical or computational lower bounds since a lower bound for the special case (assuming
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
) is necessarily a lower bound for the more general case. How to appropriately pre-whiten, however, is an important practical aspect for ICA. To fix ideas, we shall nonetheless follow the convention and assume that
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
with the exception of Section
3.4
where we specifically address the issue of prewhitening and its implications.

Note that any signed permutation of the columns of a mixing matrix leads to essentially the same ICA representation, and one cannot differentiate between an orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{A}
and its signed permutation based on
\mathbf{X}
. To explicitly account for such ambiguity in measuring the quality of an estimate of
\mathbf{A}
, the following loss function is commonly used:

\ell_{M}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=\min_{\pi:[d]\to[d]}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sin\angle(\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{b}_{\pi(k)})

where the minimum is taken over all permutations,
\angle(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is the angle between two vectors
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
taking value in
[0,\pi/2]
, and
\mathbf{a}_{k}
and
\mathbf{b}_{k}
are the
k
th column vectors of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
respectively. In the context of ICA,
\ell_{M}
measures how poorly each “unmixing directions”
\mathbf{a}_{j}
is estimated since
S_{j}=\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
,
j=1,2,\ldots,d
. Alternatively, we can also look at the quality of an estimate in an averaged sense:

\ell_{A}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=\min_{\pi}\left({1\over d}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d}\sin^{2}\angle(\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{b}_{\pi(k)})\right)^{1/2}.

Both loss functions quantify the overall accuracy of the estimated “unmixing directions”. Our first result gives a minimax lower bound for estimating
\mathbf{A}
:

Theorem 2.1
.

For any
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\inf_{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})\\
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[\ell(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A})]\geq c\cdot\min\left\{\sqrt{d\over n},1\right\},

where the loss function
\ell
is either
\ell_{M}
or
\ell_{A}
defined above and the infimum is taken over all estimators of
\mathbf{A}
based on observing
n
independent copies
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
of
\mathbf{X}
.

It is worth noting that the lower bound given in Theorem
2.1
holds for any
\epsilon>0
. In other words, one cannot hope to improve it with a higher moment (larger
\epsilon
) requirement for the sources. In fact, what we proved is slightly stronger than stated here: the lower bound holds even if all source variables are subgaussian.

The above minimax lower bound can be equivalently expressed in terms of the sample complexity commonly used in the literature. Specifically, to ensure that there exists an estimate
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
satisfying
\ell(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A})\leq_{p}\varepsilon
, we need a sample size
n\gtrsim d/\varepsilon^{2}
. This sample complexity is much smaller than those established for the state-of-the-art ICA techniques. See, e.g.,
Anandkumar et al. 2014c
;
Belkin et al. 2018
. Naturally, one asks whether this sample size requirement is indeed attainable. The answer is affirmative, if doubtful at the first glance, and we shall introduce an estimate that attains the optimal sample complexity later in the section. Our approach is motivated by a close inspection of the connection between ICA and moment estimation underlying many existing ICA techniques. Our investigation starts by revealing a fundamental yet subtle limitation of how this connection is commonly exploited, and hence why the earlier approaches are necessarily suboptimal.

2.2
ICA and Moment Estimation

Many popular ICA methods are based upon the crucial observation that, if
\mathbf{X}
is centered and pre-whitened so that its covariance matrix is the identity matrix, then the column vectors,
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{d}
, of
\mathbf{A}
can be identified with linear transforms of
\mathbf{X}
that optimize the kurtosis. A useful way to understand this property is through the decomposition of the fourth-order cumulant tenor of
\mathbf{X}
.

Recall that
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}
with
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
. Since
\{S_{j}:1\leq j\leq d\}
are independent random variables, the characteristic function of
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be written as

\phi(\mathbf{v})=\mathbb{E}\exp(i\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{X})=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\exp(i(\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j})S_{j})=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\phi_{j}(\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j})

for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here
\mathbf{a}_{j}
is the
j
th column of
\mathbf{A}
and
\phi_{j}(\cdot)
is the characteristic function of
S_{j}
. This implies that the cumulant generating function of
\mathbf{X}
can be expressed as

\psi(\mathbf{v}):=\log\phi(\mathbf{v})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\log(\phi_{j}(\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j}))=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\psi_{j}(\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j}),

(1)

where
\psi_{j}(\cdot)=\log(\phi_{j}(\cdot))
is the cumulant generating function of
S_{j}
. Differentiating both sides of the equation (
1
), four times with respect to
\mathbf{v}
, one has

\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\kappa_{4}(S_{j})(\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{4}=(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})\times_{1}\mathbf{v}\times_{2}\mathbf{v}\times_{3}\mathbf{v}\times_{4}\mathbf{v},

where

\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{X}\circ\mathbf{X}\circ\mathbf{X}\circ\mathbf{X})

and

\mathscr{M}_{0}=\sum_{\{i,j,k,l\}=\{i_{1},i_{2}\}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{d}\mathbf{e}_{i}\circ\mathbf{e}_{j}\circ\mathbf{e}_{k}\circ\mathbf{e}_{l}.

(2)

Hereafter
\circ
represents the outer product, i.e., the
(i,j,k,l)
entry of
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
is
[\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})]_{ijkl}=\mathbb{E}(X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}X_{l})
,
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is the
i
th canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\times_{j}
means multiplication between a tensor along the
j
th mode and a vector of conformable dimension.

The expression above holds for all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and hence,
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}
, the fourth cumulant tensor of
\mathbf{X}
, is orthogonally decomposable (ODECO):

\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\kappa_{4}(S_{j})\mathbf{a}_{j}\circ\mathbf{a}_{j}\circ\mathbf{a}_{j}\circ\mathbf{a}_{j}.

(3)

We shall refer to
\mathbf{a}_{j}
s and
\kappa_{4}(S_{j})
s as the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}
. As such, the
\mathbf{a}_{j}
s are the complete enumeration of the local optima of
f(\mathbf{u}):=\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})
with respect to the domain
{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
. This, in particular, implies that random initialization followed by gradient iterations can recover
\mathbf{a}_{j}
s with probability one. See, e.g.,
Belkin et al. 2018
for detailed discussions. Inspired by this observation, many approaches to ICA aim at finding the local optima of the sample kurtosis:

\widehat{f}(\mathbf{u}):=\widehat{\kappa}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})=(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})\times_{1}\mathbf{u}\times_{2}\mathbf{u}\times_{3}\mathbf{u}\times_{4}\mathbf{u}

where

\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})={1\over n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{X}_{i}\circ\mathbf{X}_{i}\circ\mathbf{X}_{i}\circ\mathbf{X}_{i}),

is the fourth-order sample moment tensor. For example, the popular FastICA can be viewed as a fixed-point algorithm to optimize
\widehat{f}
over
{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
.

It is important to note that, even though
f
only has
d
local optima,
\widehat{f}
may have many more. The success of any algorithm hinges upon its ability to find the local optima of
\widehat{f}
that are close to those of
f
. Clearly, regardless of which algorithm to use, how well this strategy works depends on how well the sample kurtosis approximates its population counterpart. Perhaps, somewhat surprisingly, the following result indicates that, no matter which algorithm is used to optimize
\widehat{f}
, the resulting estimating procedure cannot achieve the optimal sample complexity given by Theorem
2.1
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
are
n
independent copies of a random vector
\mathbf{X}
such that
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and constants
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
. There exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that if
n\leq C_{1}d^{2}
then,

\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\widehat{\kappa}_{4}^{\rm sample}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})-\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})|\geq 4

with probability at least
1-d^{-4}
. Moreover,

\max_{1\leq j\leq d}|\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\widehat{\mathbf{u}}\rangle|\leq C_{2}\sqrt{d^{-1/4}\log d},

with probability at least
1-n^{-\epsilon/8}
, where

\widehat{\mathbf{u}}\in\argmax_{\mathbf{u}:\|\mathbf{u}\|=1}\widehat{f}(\mathbf{u}).

The first statement of Theorem
2.2
suggests that the sample kurtosis does not approximate the true kurtosis well for some
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and hence maximizing the sample kurtosis may not lead to consistent estimates of the maximizer of the population kurtosis. Indeed, the second statement states more precisely how maximizing the sample kurtosis fails to recover any of the unmixing directions when
n\ll d^{2}
: even if we can overcome the nonconvexity of
\widehat{f}
and optimize it, the optimizer is not a good estimate. More specifically, when
d
diverges,

\max_{1\leq j\leq d}|\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\widehat{\mathbf{u}}\rangle|\to_{p}0.

In other words,
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}
behaves like the worst possible estimate of
\mathbf{a}_{j}
s as it is asymptotically orthogonal to all of them! It is worth noting that this peculiar phenomenon is unique in a high dimensional situation – although
\{\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{d}\}
is a complete basis for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the vector
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}
can still be nearly perpendicular to all of them.

Theorem
2.2
indicates that any ICA algorithm aiming to optimize the sample kurtosis, if performing as desired, cannot be consistent when
n\ll d^{2}
, and thus is not minimax optimal at least in the regime
d\ll n\ll d^{2}
. This limitation is due to the inefficiencies of sample kurtosis and thankfully can be overcome with an improved moment estimate. As a result, we shall argue, the lower bound in Theorem
2.1
is indeed attainable.

2.3
Minimax Optimal ICA

The main idea is to first construct a more reliable estimate of
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}:=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{4}
separately for a carefully chosen set of
\mathbf{u}
s, and then use them as anchors to derive an improved estimate of
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}
for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. We now describe the procedure in detail.

Estimating
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}
for a fixed
\mathbf{u}
.

We first discuss how to estimate
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}
for a fixed
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Note that this amounts to estimating the mean of
(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{4}
and we shall appeal to a general strategy developed by
Catoni 2012
. More specifically, with a slight abuse of notation, let
\psi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a non-decreasing influence function such that

-\log(1-x+x^{2}/2)\leq\psi(x)\leq\log(1+x+x^{2}/2).

Denote by
\psi_{\alpha}(x)=\psi(\alpha x)
. See
Catoni 2012
for more concrete examples of the influence function
\psi
. For any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, denote by
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathbf{u}}
the solution to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\alpha}\left\{(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i})^{4}-{\theta}_{\mathbf{u}}\right\}=0.

(4)

Under the moment assumptions for the sources, the results from
Catoni 2012
imply that, for any
t>\exp(-n/2)
,

\left|\widehat{\theta}_{\mathbf{u}}-\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i})^{4}\right|\leq\sqrt{2M_{2}^{8/(8+\epsilon)}\log(t^{-1})\over n\left(1-2\log(t^{-1})/n\right)},

(5)

with probability at least
1-2t
. In other words, for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathbf{u}}
has subgaussian tails.

Estimating
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}
for all
\mathbf{u}
s.

Now let
{\cal N}
be a
1/4
covering set of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with
|{\cal N}|\leq 9^{d}
. We first estimate
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}
for every
\mathbf{u}\in{\cal N}
as above and then use them as anchors to construct estimates for
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}
for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Recall that
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}=\langle\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}),\mathbf{u}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{u}\rangle
. Estimating
\theta_{\mathbf{u}}
, therefore, amounts to estimating the moment tensor
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
. To this end, we shall take advantage of the key property that we noticed in the previous subsection:
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}
is ODECO. Specifically, we shall estimate
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
by

\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X}):=\argmin_{\mathscr{A}:\mathscr{A}-\mathscr{M}_{0}\text{ is ODECO}}\max_{\mathbf{u}\in{\cal N}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{\mathbf{u}}-\langle\mathscr{A},\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{u}\rangle\right|.

(6)

Estimating the mixing matrix.

By definition,
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}
is an ODECO tensor and we can then estimate
\mathbf{a}_{j}
s by its singular vectors. Denote by

\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{\lambda}_{j}\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}.

(7)

where the singular vectors
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}
s are also the stationary points of

\widehat{\kappa}_{4}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})=\langle\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0},\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{u}\rangle.

(8)

The following theorem shows that
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}
s are indeed minimax optimal estimates of the column vectors of
\mathbf{A}
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
are
n
independent copies of a random vector
\mathbf{X}
such that
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
. There exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that if
n>C_{1}d
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-d)
,

\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\widehat{\kappa}_{4}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})-\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})|\leq C_{2}\sqrt{d\over n},

where
\widehat{\kappa}_{4}
is defined by (
8
). And consequently, with probability at least
1-\exp(-d)
,

\ell(\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{A})\leq C_{2}\sqrt{d\over n}.

where the loss function
\ell
is either
\ell_{M}
or
\ell_{A}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}=[\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{d}]
, and
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{j}
s are defined by (
7
).

Although the estimate we introduced above is minimax optimal, it also incurs prohibitive computational costs when
d
is large. It not only requires estimating the kurtosis for an exponential (in
d
) number of
\mathbf{u}
s, but also involves a highly nonconvex optimization problem of (
7
). This naturally brings about the question: are there alternative approaches that are both minimax optimal and computationally tractable? The answer turns out to be mixed: we can but only with a more stringent sample size requirement. Specifically, we shall first argue that if we restrict our attention to a large class of computationally efficient algorithms, then the minimum sample size required for consistent estimation is at least of the order
d^{2}
, up to a logarithmic factor, rather than linear in
d
as Theorem
2.1
suggests.

2.4
Computational Limits for ICA

We now turn our attention to estimates that can be computed using a general class of polynomial-time (in
d
) algorithms. In particular, we make use of the recently developed low-degree polynomial bounds. The low-degree polynomial method originated from the sum-of-squares literature in theoretical computer science and has since been developed into a general framework to study the average-case computational complexity. See, e.g.,
Hopkins 2018
or
Kunisky et al. 2022
for further details. Many popular algorithms such as power iteration or approximate message passing can be cast within this framework. It has been shown to provide a unified explanation of information-computation gaps in a number of important statistical problems including sparse PCA, tensor PCA, and planted submatrix among many others in the sense that the best-known polynomial-time computable algorithms are low-degree polynomials and conversely low-degree polynomial algorithms fail in the “hard” regime.

In the setting of ICA, a low-degree polynomial algorithm takes as input
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
, and produces an output, an estimate of the mixing matrix, that can be expressed as a polynomial of the input of degree
O(\log d)
. Note that the dimension of a space of polynomials up to a certain degree grows exponentially with the degree so that the space of polynomials of degree
O(\log d)
has dimension
d^{O(1)}
.
Our next result shows that if we restrict our attention to this class of algorithms, sample size
n\gg d^{2}/{\rm polylog}(d)
is required to ensure consistency of the estimated mixing matrix.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\mathbb{R}_{\leq D}[\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}]
be the set of all real polynomials of degree up to
D
over independent copies
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
of
\mathbf{X}
. Suppose that
D\leq c_{0}\log d
for some constant
c_{0}>0
. Then, for any
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}
such that

\inf_{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq D}[\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}]}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})\\
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[\ell_{M}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A})]\geq c_{1},

whenever
n\leq c_{2}d^{2}(\log d)^{-10}
.

We outline here the main idea behind the proof of Theorem
2.4
. As often done in establishing minimax lower bounds, the proof of Theorem
2.4
proceeds by first reducing the problem of estimating
\mathbf{A}
to a related hypothesis testing problem about
\mathbf{A}
. Specifically, we consider testing

H_{0}:\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{P}\quad\text{vs.}\quad H_{1}:\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{Q}

based on observing
n
independent observations from the ICA model
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}
where
\mathbf{P}\neq\mathbf{Q}\in{\cal O}(d)
are two fixed orthonormal matrices. The idea is that if we can not differentiate between
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
, then we cannot estimate
\mathbf{A}
up to an error of
\ell(\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q})/2
.

Without the computational constraint, we can consistently differentiate between
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
if the likelihood ratio diverges. Intuitively, if only low-degree polynomial algorithms are allowed, then the low-degree polynomial projection of the likelihood ratio must diverge. The projection of the likelihood ratio onto the space of low-degree polynomials is given by

\Lambda_{\leq D}:=\max_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq D}[\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}]}\dfrac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{Q}}f(\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n})}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{P}}f^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n})}}.

(9)

See, e.g.,
Hopkins 2018
. The above optimization over all possible polynomials has a solution in terms of the ratio of the likelihood functions of
\mathbf{X}
under
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
. Let
\mu
and
\nu
be the laws of
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{S}
respectively. We assume that both
\mu
and
\nu
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and write the likelihood ratio under these two measures as

L(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n})=\dfrac{L_{\nu}(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n})}{L_{\mu}(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n})}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{d}\dfrac{f_{k}(\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i})}{f_{k}(\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i})}

where
f_{k}
is the density of
S_{k}
. With
\underline{\mathbf{X}}=(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n})
, it can be shown that

\Lambda^{2}_{\leq D}=\sum_{t=1}^{N}\langle L,b_{t}\rangle^{2}=\sum_{t=1}^{N}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\otimes n}}\left(L(\underline{\mathbf{X}})b_{t}(\underline{\mathbf{X}})\right)\right)^{2}=\sum_{t=1}^{N}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\otimes n}}\left(b_{t}(\underline{\mathbf{X}})\right)\right)^{2},

(10)

where
\{b_{t}\}_{t=1}^{N}
is a basis for polynomials of degree at most
D
, and the last equality follows by the definition of the likelihood ratio. By choosing an appropriate basis
\{b_{t}\}_{t=1}^{N}
, we can show that

Lemma 1
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be independent copies of
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}
. Suppose
D\leq c_{0}\log d
for some constant
c_{0}>0
. Then there is a constant
C>0
such that for any
\mathbf{P}\in{\cal O}(d)
, there exists a
\mathbf{Q}\in{\cal O}(d)
such that

\ell_{M}(\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q})=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\quad{\rm and}\quad\mathbb{E}\Lambda_{\leq D}\leq 2,

(11)

whenever
n\leq d^{2}/(CD^{2}(\log d)^{10})
.

We can then leverage Lemma
1
to prove Theorem
2.4
. To this end, suppose we have an estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{1}
of the first column of
\mathbf{A}
that can be expressed as a polynomial of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
with order up to
D
. For
\mathbf{P}\in{\cal O}(d)
, consider the low-degree polynomial

g(\underline{\mathbf{X}})=(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{n})^{4}-(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{n})^{4}.

Intuitively, if
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{1}
is a good estimate, say,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{A}}\left(|\langle\widehat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a}_{1}\rangle|>0.9\right)\geq 0.9
, then we shall be able to tell
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
apart based on the value of
g(\underline{\mathbf{X}})
, and consequently, ensure that the ratio
{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{Q}}g(\underline{\mathbf{X}})}/{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{P}}g^{2}(\underline{\mathbf{X}})}}
is sufficiently large. This, however, contradicts Lemma
1
which states that it cannot be larger than 2.

3
Computationally Tractable ICA

In light of the discussion from the previous section, we shall focus on the case when
n\gtrsim d^{2}
and develop a computationally tractable ICA approach that is minimax optimal in this regime. The main challenge for ICA when
d
is large is the fact that
\widehat{f}
is highly nonconvex and can have many more than
d
local optima most of which are not close to any of the
\mathbf{a}_{j}
s. There are two general strategies to overcome this challenge. One is to incorporate occasional jumps to the iterations to make it possible to escape a bad local optimum. See, e.g.,
Belkin et al. 2018
. The other is to ensure the initialization is sufficiently close to a good local optimum. In particular, our approach is closely related to and inspired by a careful inspection of the popular FastICA algorithm. To this end, we shall first briefly review the FastICA procedure and highlight its perils and challenges when
d
is large.

3.1
FastICA and Its Pitfalls when
d
is Large

As noted, FastICA is a fixed-point algorithm to optimize the sample kurtosis:

\widehat{f}(\mathbf{u})=\widehat{\kappa}_{4}^{\rm sample}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X})={1\over n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i})^{4}-3,

over
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
. The algorithm consists of three parts: initialization which is typically sampled uniformly from the sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
, fixed-point iterations either with a prespecified number of iterations or until a certain convergence criterion is met, and deflation where an estimated independent component is removed by projecting the observations onto its orthogonal complement. These steps are repeated until all independent components are recovered. See
Hyvärinen and Oja 2000
for details.

Algorithm 1

FastICA

Input:

\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}

1:

for

j=1
to
d

do

2:

\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}^{[0]}\leftarrow

initialize

// Initialization

3:

for

t=1
to
T

do

// Fixed-point iteration

4:

\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}^{[t-1]}-{1\over 3n}\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\mathbf{X}_{i}((\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}^{[t-1]})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i})^{3}]

5:

\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}^{[t]}\leftarrow{\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}\over\|\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}\|}

6:

\mathbf{X}_{i}\leftarrow\mathbf{X}_{i}-((\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}^{[t]})^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i})\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}^{[t]},\quad i=1,\ldots,n

// Deflation

7:

return

\{\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}^{[T]}:1\leq j\leq d\}
.

A good initialization is arguably the most critical to the success of the algorithm because
\widehat{f}(\cdot)
may have exponentially many local optima most of which are not necessarily close to any of the
\mathbf{a}_{j}
s. Indeed, the fact that initialization plays an important role in FastICA is widely known in practice. When
d
is small, this issue can be resolved by running the FastICA algorithm with multiple random initializations to ensure that we start from somewhere close to a mixing direction at least for one try. However, the difficulty is exacerbated with a larger
d
, and this simple strategy may no longer be practical. To see this, note that a uniformly sampled direction
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
satisfies

\max_{1\leq k\leq d}|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle|=O_{p}(\sqrt{d^{-1}\log d}).

For small
d
, this means that there exists a direction
\mathbf{a}_{k}
such that
|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle|
is bounded away from 0. But, when
d
is large, this is no longer the case and
\mathbf{u}
will be nearly orthogonal to all
\mathbf{a}_{k}
s!

This problem, unfortunately, cannot be easily resolved with multiple rounds of random initialization. Consider repeatedly sampling
L
directions
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{L}
. An application of the union bound yields

\max_{1\leq l\leq L}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}|\langle\mathbf{u}_{l},\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle|=O_{p}(\sqrt{d^{-1}(\log d+\log L)}).

Thus, it is going to take exponentially many, i.e.,
\exp(\Omega(d))
, random initializations to ensure that at least one
\mathbf{u}_{l}
has a nonvanishing inner product with one of the mixing directions, which makes it computationally infeasible.

Another common strategy of initialization for ICA is via suitable matricization of the cumulant tensor. See, e.g.,
Hyvärinen and Oja 2000
. Specifically, one collapses the first two modes and the last two modes of
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}
to form a
d^{2}\times d^{2}
matrix
{\cal M}_{(1,2)(3,4)}(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})
. Let
{\cal M}_{(1,2)(3,4)}(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})=UDV^{\top}
be its singular value decomposition and denote by
\mathbf{u}_{1}
the first column vector of
U
. Next
\mathbf{u}_{1}
is reshaped into a
d\times d
matrix, denoted by
U_{1}
. We set the initial value
\mathbf{a}_{1}^{[0]}
to be the leading left singular vector of
U_{1}
. The rationale behind this approach is that
\mathscr{M}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}
estimates

\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\kappa_{4}(S_{k})\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}.

Note that
\mathbf{a}_{k}\otimes\mathbf{a}_{k}
s are the eigenvectors of
{\cal M}_{(1,2)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})
:

{\cal M}_{(1,2)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\kappa_{4}(S_{k})(\mathbf{a}_{k}\otimes\mathbf{a}_{k})\circ(\mathbf{a}_{k}\otimes\mathbf{a}_{k}),

(12)

where
\otimes
represents the Kronecker product. However, the eigenvectors of
{\cal M}_{(1,2)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})
are uniquely defined only if
\kappa_{4}(S_{k})
s are distinct. Even if this is true, the validity of this approach will depend on the differences among
\kappa_{4}(S_{k})
s relative to the estimation error
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
. All of these impose unnecessary and sometimes unrealistic assumptions on the sources.

In addition to the initialization, when
d
is more than a handful, one also needs to be concerned with the computational complexity of any ICA algorithm: how does it scale with an increasing dimensionality and how many iterations are required to ensure a certain precision even if we start with a “good” initial value? Of course, the situation gets even more involved as we sequentially recover the independent components. As each unmixing direction is estimated and removed through deflation, how their estimation error accumulates and affects the estimation of remaining directions is essential to understand if the algorithm works for moderate or large
d
. Also pertinent is the statistical performance of the final estimates: perhaps the most important distinction from earlier analysis of this type of iterative algorithm is that we prove that the unmixing directions can be estimated at the minimax optimal rate of convergence that is far superior to the estimated kurtosis.

We shall discuss how all these challenges can be addressed through careful analyses and propose an improved estimating procedure that is both computationally tractable and statistically efficient.

3.2
Initialization

The goal of initialization is to start with a nontrivial estimate
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}^{[0]}
. More specifically, we want to make sure that
\sin\angle(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}^{[0]},\mathbf{a}_{k})
is bounded away from
1
. Two main ideas behind our proposal is random slicing and improved moment estimation which we shall now describe in detail.

3.2.1
Random Slicing

We begin with random slicing. The idea of random slicing is commonly used for tensor decomposition. See, e.g.,
Anandkumar et al. 2014c
. We shall take a closer look at its operating characteristics which, interestingly, suggest existing implementations of the random slicing are suboptimal, before discussing ways to improve them.

Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
be an estimate of
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
and write
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})=\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})+\mathscr{E}
where
\mathscr{E}
is its estimation error. Suppose that
G
is a
d\times d
random matrix whose entries are independently drawn from the standard normal distribution. Then
(\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})\times_{3,4}G
is a random combination of the
(1,2)
slices of the estimated cumulant tenor
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0}
:

(\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})\times_{3,4}G=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\kappa_{4}(S_{k})(\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}G\mathbf{a}_{k})\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}+\mathscr{E}\times_{3,4}G.

(13)

Eigengap in the signal.

The first part on the right-hand side of (
13
) represents the signal that we wish to recover. It has eigenvalues
\{\kappa_{4}(S_{k})(\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}G\mathbf{a}_{k}):1\leq k\leq d\}
. Because of the randomness in
G
, even if all
\kappa_{4}(S_{k})
s are equal, these eigenvalues are necessarily different. Moreover, we can create a large eigengap between the top two singular values of the signal through multiple rounds of random slicing. More specifically, suppose that we repeat the random slicing
L
times with independently generated Gaussian random matrix
G_{1},\ldots,G_{L}
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})|\geq|\kappa_{4}(S_{k})|
for all
k>1
. The “eigenvalues” corresponding to
\mathbf{a}_{1}
are
\{\kappa_{4}(S_{1})(\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\top}G_{l}\mathbf{a}_{1}):1\leq l\leq L\}
. These are independent normal random variables so that

\max_{1\leq l\leq L}|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})(\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\top}G_{l}\mathbf{a}_{1})|=|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})|\sqrt{2\log L}(1+o_{p}(1)).

(14)

Denote by
L^{\ast}
be the maximizing index. Note that for any
k>1
,
\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}G_{L^{\ast}}\mathbf{a}_{k}
is independent of
\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\top}G_{L^{\ast}}\mathbf{a}_{1}
. Therefore

\max_{k>1}|\kappa_{4}(S_{k})(\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}G_{L^{\ast}}\mathbf{a}_{k})|=\max_{k>1}|\kappa_{4}(S_{k})|\sqrt{2\log d}(1+o_{p}(1)).

If we take
L\geq d^{2}
, then for the
L^{\ast}
round of random slicing, the leading singular value of the first term on the rightmost-hand side of (
13
) is
2|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})|\sqrt{\log d}(1+o_{p}(1))
whereas its second largest singular value is no bigger than
|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})|\sqrt{2\log d}(1+o_{p}(1))
, and therefore creating an eigengap
(2-\sqrt{2})|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})|(\log d)^{1/2}(1+o_{p}(1))
.

Error bounds for the noise.

The second term on the right-hand side of (
13
) is the “noise” that we wish to be able to control. Write
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{E}_{1}+\mathscr{E}_{2}
where

\mathscr{E}_{1}=(\mathscr{E}\times_{3}\mathbf{a}_{1}\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{1})\circ\mathbf{a}_{1}\circ\mathbf{a}_{1}.

It is easy to see that

\|\mathscr{E}_{1}\times_{3,4}G_{L^{\ast}}\|=|\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\top}G_{L^{\ast}}\mathbf{a}_{1}|\|\mathscr{E}\times_{3}\mathbf{a}_{1}\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{1}\|\leq|\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\top}G_{L^{\ast}}\mathbf{a}_{1}|\|\mathscr{E}\|,

where, as before,
L^{\ast}
is the slicing that maximizes the left-hand side of (
14
). Therefore we can make sure it is dominated by
|\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\top}G_{L^{\ast}}\mathbf{a}_{1}|
if
\|\mathscr{E}\|
is made sufficiently small.

On the other hand, conditional on the observations
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
,

\mathscr{E}_{2}\times_{3,4}G_{L^{\ast}}=\mathscr{E}_{2}\times_{3,4}(\mathbb{I}_{d}-\mathbf{a}_{1}\circ\mathbf{a}_{1})G_{L^{\ast}}

is again independent of
\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\top}G_{L^{\ast}}\mathbf{a}_{1}
. An application of concentration inequality for matrix Gaussian sequence from
Tropp et al. 2015
yields, with probability at least
1-e^{-t^{2}/2}
,

\|\mathscr{E}_{2}\times_{3,4}G_{L^{\ast}}\|\leq\|{\cal M}_{(1,3,4)(2)}(\mathscr{E}_{2})\|(\sqrt{2\log d}+t)\leq\|{\cal M}_{(1,3,4)(2)}(\mathscr{E})\|(\sqrt{2\log d}+t),

assuming that
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}
is symmetric. Here, similar to before,
{\cal M}_{(1,3,4)(2)}(\cdot)
denotes the reshaping of fourth-order tensor into a matrix by collapsing its first, third, and fourth indices.

Together, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{E}\times_{3,4}G_{L^{\ast}}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{E}_{1}\times_{3,4}G_{L^{\ast}}\|+\|\mathscr{E}_{2}\times_{3,4}G_{L^{\ast}}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\log L}\|\mathscr{E}\|(1+o_{p}(1))+\|{\cal M}_{(1,3,4)(2)}(\mathscr{E})\|(\sqrt{2\log d}+O_{p}(1)).

Conditions for moment estimation.

If we can make sure that

\|\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}-\mathscr{M}_{4}\|\leq c|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})|,\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\|{\cal M}_{(1,3,4)(2)}(\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}-\mathscr{M}_{4})\|\leq c|\kappa_{4}(S_{1})|

(15)

for a sufficiently small constant
c>0
, then an application of the Davis-Kahn-Wedin Theorem immediately yields that
\mathbf{u}_{L^{\ast}}
is a nontrivial estimate of
\mathbf{a}_{1}
in that
\sin\angle(\mathbf{u}_{L^{\ast}},\mathbf{a}_{1})
can be bounded away from 1, where
\mathbf{u}_{L^{\ast}}
is the leading singular vector of
(\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})\times_{3,4}G_{L^{\ast}}
. This brings our attention back to moment estimation.

3.2.2
Moment Estimation Revisited

An obvious choice for
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{4}
in our previous discussion is the sample moment tensor
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}^{\rm sample}(\mathbf{X})
. Indeed it can be shown to obey the first inequality in (
15
) when
n\gtrsim d^{2}
. More specifically,

Lemma 2
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
such that
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and constants
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
.Then there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, for any
t_{1},t_{2}>0
,

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}^{\rm sample}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})\right\rVert\geq C_{1}(t_{1}+t_{2})\sqrt{\dfrac{d^{2}}{n}}

with probability at most
\exp(-C_{2}n^{\epsilon/8})+\exp(-C_{2}t_{1})+(C_{2}n^{\epsilon/8}t_{2}^{2+\epsilon/4})^{-1}
.

However, a more careful inspection reveals that the second condition of (
15
) does not hold for the sample moment tensor unless
n\gtrsim d^{3}
. This is because direct matricization of the sample moment tensor can induce significant bias.

To illustrate this point, let us consider estimating
{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))
. Note that it is also the second moment matrix of
\mathbf{Y}:=\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{X}
, i.e.,

{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))=\texttt{cov}(\mathbf{Y})+\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y})^{\top}=\texttt{cov}(\mathbf{Y})+\texttt{vec}(\mathbb{I}_{d})\texttt{vec}(\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\top}.

Likewise,
{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))
is also the sample moment of
\mathbf{Y}
and amounts to estimating both the covariance and expectation of
\mathbf{Y}
by their sample counterpart. In particular, estimating
\texttt{vec}(\mathbb{I}_{d})\texttt{vec}(\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\top}
by
\bar{\mathbf{Y}}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}^{\top}
incurs a dominating and unnecessary estimation error of the order
\sqrt{d^{3}/n}
. Instead, a better estimate of
{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))
is

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}:={1\over n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{Y}_{i}-\bar{\mathbf{Y}})(\mathbf{Y}_{i}-\bar{\mathbf{Y}})^{\top}+\texttt{vec}(\mathbb{I}_{d})\texttt{vec}(\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\top}.

(16)

Lemma 3
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of a random vector
\mathbf{X}
such that
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and constants
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
.Then there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, for any
t_{1},t_{2}>0
,

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathbf{M}}-{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\right\rVert\geq C_{1}(t_{1}+t_{2})\sqrt{\dfrac{d^{2}}{n}}

with probability at most
\exp(-C_{2}n^{\epsilon/8})+\exp(-C_{2}t_{1})+(C_{2}n^{\epsilon/8}t_{2}^{2+\epsilon/4})^{-1}
.

In principle, we can view
{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbf{M}})
as an estimate of the moment tensor
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
. However, it is designed for estimating
{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))
and can be further improved for estimating other matricizations of
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
. The main idea is the observation that
{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})
in (
12
) is a
d^{2}\times d^{2}
matrix but has rank
d
. In the following, we use a sample splitting idea to utilize this additional information.

To this end, we divide the samples into
{\cal S}_{1}
and
{\cal S}_{2}
of size
n/2
. We then compute
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{2}
as above using the samples in
{\cal S}_{1}
and
{\cal S}_{2}
respectively. Let
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}
be the projection matrix onto the top-
d
singular space of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{2}-{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{0})
. We shall proceed to estimate
\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
by

\widehat{\mathscr{M}}={\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbf{P}}(\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{1}-{\cal M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{0}))\widehat{\mathbf{P}})+\mathscr{M}_{0}.

(17)

The following lemma shows that the moment estimate
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}
satisfies the condition (
15
).

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of a random vector
\mathbf{X}
such that
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and constants
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
. Then there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, for any
t_{1},t_{2}>0
,

\max\left\{\left\|\widehat{\mathscr{M}}-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})\right\|,\left\|{\cal M}_{(123)(4)}(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\right\|\right\}\geq C_{1}(t_{1}+t_{2})\sqrt{\dfrac{d^{2}}{n}}

with probability at most
\exp(-C_{2}n^{\epsilon/8})+\exp(-C_{2}t_{1})+(C_{2}n^{\epsilon/8}t_{2}^{2+\epsilon/4})^{-1}
.

3.2.3
Initialization

The following algorithm summarizes our proposed initialization scheme.

Algorithm 2

Intialization

Input:

\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}

1:

Divide the samples into disjoint sets
{\cal S}_{1}
and
{\cal S}_{2}
of size
n/2
.

2:

Compute
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{2}
as defined by (
16
) on
{\cal S}_{1}
and
{\cal S}_{2}
respectively.

3:

Compute the top rank-
d
projection
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}
of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{2}-\mathcal{M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{0})
.

4:

Compute the cumulant tensor estimate
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}
as defined by (
17
)

5:

for

l=1
to
L

do

6:

Generate a
d\times d
Gaussian random matrix
G_{l}

7:

Compute the leading singular value and left singular vector of
(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}-\mathcal{M}_{(12)(3,4)}(\mathscr{M}_{0}))\times_{3,4}G_{l}
, denoted by
\sigma_{l}
and
\mathbf{u}_{l}

8:

Compute
L^{\ast}=\argmax_{1\leq l\leq L}\sigma_{l}
.

9:

return

\mathbf{u}_{L^{\ast}}
.

The following theorem shows that Algorithm
2
indeed yields a nontrivial estimate of one of the column vectors of
\mathbf{A}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of a random vector
\mathbf{X}
such that
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and constants
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
, and
\mathbf{u}
the output from Algorithm
2
with
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
as input. Then there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\min_{1\leq k\leq d}\sin\angle\left(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{a}_{k}\right)\leq\dfrac{1}{4}

with probability at least
1-C_{1}d^{-3}
, provided that
n>C_{2}d^{2}
and
L>C_{2}d^{2}
.

It is worth noting that thus far our discussion has centered around initializing the first direction. For the remaining directions, we need to apply the algorithm to deflated observations. Because of the error associated with the already estimated directions, the input to Algorithm
2
does not necessarily follow an ICA model. Nonetheless, as we shall see later that with a sufficient number of fixed-point iterations and appropriate deflation, Algorithm
2
can be used successively to yield good initialization for all columns vectors of
\mathbf{A}
.

3.3
Convergence Analysis

Now that we have a nontrivial initialization, we turn our attention to the complexity of fixed-point iteration and the effect of deflation. A careful inspection of the dynamics of these steps reveal that we can estimate
\mathbf{a}_{k}
s at the minimax optimal rate of
\sqrt{d/n}
which is much faster than rate
\sqrt{d^{2}/n}
for estimating the moment tensor under the operator norm.

3.3.1
Fixed-point iteration

We begin with the fixed-point iterations. We shall focus on recovering the first mixing direction to illustrate the main ideas. Following the discussion in the previous subsection, we shall from now on assume that we have an initial value
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[0]}
obeying

\min_{1\leq k\leq d}\sin\angle\left(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[0]},\mathbf{a}_{k}\right)\leq 1-\eta,

(18)

for some
\eta>0
. Assume, without loss of generality, that the minimizer of left-hand side is
k=1
for brevity. We now investigate how the fixed-point iterations could lead to a rate optimal estimate of
\mathbf{a}_{1}
. Particular attention needs to be paid to how different errors of the sample moment tensor may affect the dynamics of the iteration.

Recall that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t]}

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{0})\times_{2}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{3}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{4}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}\kappa_{4}(S_{j})\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\rangle^{3}\mathbf{a}_{j}+(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{2}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{3}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{4}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}.

Denote by
\eta_{t}=\sin\angle(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t]},\mathbf{a}_{j})
. Then

\left|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})\langle\mathbf{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\rangle^{3}\right|=|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})|(1-\eta_{t-1}^{2})^{3/2}

and

\left\|\sum_{l\neq j}\kappa_{4}(S_{l})\langle\mathbf{a}_{l},\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\rangle^{3}\mathbf{a}_{l}\right\|=\left(\sum_{l\neq j}\kappa_{4}^{2}(S_{l})\langle\mathbf{a}_{l},\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\rangle^{6}\right)^{1/2}\leq M_{1}\eta_{t-1}^{3}.

On the other hand, by triangular inequality,

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{2}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{3}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{4}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{2}\mathbf{a}_{j}\times_{3}\mathbf{a}_{j}\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|+3\eta_{t-1}\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{3}\mathbf{a}_{j}\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|

\displaystyle+3\eta_{t-1}^{2}\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|+\eta_{t-1}^{3}\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}+3\eta_{t-1}\Delta_{2}+3\eta_{t-1}^{2}\Delta_{1}+\eta_{t-1}^{3}\Delta,

where

\Delta=\|\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})\|,\qquad\Delta_{1}=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|,

\Delta_{2}=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{3}\mathbf{a}_{j}\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|,

and

\Delta_{3}=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{2}\mathbf{a}_{j}\times_{3}\mathbf{a}_{j}\times_{4}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|.

The key observation here is that the final bound depends on the estimation error of the sample moment tensors measured in various matricized norms. We shall leverage the fact that these errors are of different magnitudes to better characterize the dynamics of the fix-point iteration.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of a random vector
\mathbf{X}
such that
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and constants
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
. There exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\Delta_{1}\leq\Delta\leq C_{1}(t_{1}+t_{2})\sqrt{\dfrac{d^{2}}{n}},

and

\Delta_{3}\leq\Delta_{2}\leq C_{1}(t_{1}+t_{2})\sqrt{\dfrac{d}{n}},

with probability at least
1-\exp(-C_{2}t_{1})-(C_{2}n^{\epsilon/8}t_{2}^{2+\epsilon/4})^{-1}
.

Note that

\displaystyle\eta_{t}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\rangle^{3}\right|^{-1}\left\|\sum_{l\neq j}\kappa_{4}(S_{l})\langle\mathbf{a}_{l},\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\rangle^{3}\mathbf{a}_{l}\right\|

\displaystyle+\left|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\rangle^{3}\right|^{-1}\left\|(\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X}))\times_{2}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{3}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\times_{4}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[t-1]}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})|^{-1}\Delta_{3}+6|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})|^{-1}\Delta_{2}\eta_{t-1}+6|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})|^{-1}\Delta_{1}\eta_{t-1}^{2}+2|\kappa_{4}(S_{j})|^{-1}\Delta\eta_{t-1}^{3},

provided that
\eta_{t-1}<1/2
, say. Now Lemma
5
and Theorem
3.1
yield

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose that
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[0]}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
satisfies (
18
) with a sufficiently large
0<\eta<1
. Then if
T\geq C\log d
, for any fixed
\delta\in(0,1)
, the estimator obtained after
T
fixed-point iterations satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left\{\min_{1\leq j\leq d}\sin\angle\left(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}^{[T]},\mathbf{a}_{j}\right)\leq C\sqrt{\dfrac{d(\log(1/\delta))}{n}}\right\}\geq 1-\delta-d^{-3}-n^{-\epsilon/8}

for a constant
C>0
, provided
n\geq Cd^{2}(\log\delta)^{2}
.

In other words, the number of fixed-point iterations required scales with the dimension
d
in a logarithmic fashion. This suggests that, as long as a good initialization is provided, the computational complexity can be well managed. Of course, as before, we are only concerned with recovering one unmixing direction at this point. The situation beyond the first direction is more involved as estimation error of previously recovered directions may accumulate. To address this issue, we now turn to the deflation step.

3.3.2
Deflation

The deflation step is essential to ensure that all mixing directions can be identified sequentially. In particular, suppose that we already have estimates,
\{\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}:1\leq k<j\}
, for the
j-1
unmixing directions by running Algorithm
1
with an initialization using Algorithm
2
. Without loss of generality, assume that for
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}
is a
\sqrt{d/n}
-consistent estimate of
\mathbf{a}_{k}
for
k=1,\ldots,j-1
. We now consider the effect of deflation and how well we can estimate the next unmixing direction.

It is not hard to see that the deflation step of the FastICA amounts to the subtraction of the estimated components from
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})
:

\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}^{(j),{\rm sample}}(\mathbf{X}):=\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}\widehat{f}(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k})\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}

(19)

Note that
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}^{(j),{\rm sample}}(\mathbf{X})
can be viewed as an estimate of

\mathscr{M}_{4}^{(j)}(\mathbf{X})=\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}\kappa_{4}(S_{k})\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}=\sum_{k=j}^{d}\kappa_{4}(S_{k})\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}

The same argument for initialization and fixed-point iterations remains valid if we can bound the estimation error of
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}^{(j),{\rm sample}}(\mathbf{X})
in the same way as the original sample moment
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}^{{\rm sample}}(\mathbf{X})
, which is indeed the case.

For example, in light of the discussion from previous subsections, we can show by induction that

\left|\widehat{f}(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k})\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}-\kappa_{4}(S_{k})\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\right|=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{d\over n}\right).

By triangular inequality,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{4}^{(j),{\rm sample}}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}^{(j)}(\mathbf{X})\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathscr{M}}^{\rm sample}_{4}(\mathbf{X})-\mathscr{M}_{4}(\mathbf{X})\|+\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}\left[\widehat{f}(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k})\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}\circ\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{k}-\kappa_{4}(S_{k})\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\circ\mathbf{a}_{k}\right]\right\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(\sqrt{d^{2}\over n}\right).

Other norms of the error can also be bounded similarly, thus leading to the following conclusion.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
s.t.
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
and
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
. Suppose we run Algorithm
1
with the initialization step via Algorithm
2
, and with parameters
L\geq C_{1}d^{2}
for initialization and
T\geq C_{2}\log d
for fixed-point iteration for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Denote by
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}=[\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{d}]
the output. There exist constants
C_{3},C_{4}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{3}d^{2}(\log\delta)^{2}
, then

\ell(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A})\leq C_{4}\sqrt{{d\log(1/\delta)}\over n},

with probability at least
1-\delta-d^{-3}-n^{-\epsilon/8}
with the loss function
\ell
being either
\ell_{M}
or
\ell_{A}
.

The rate of convergence given by Theorem
3.3
matches the information-theoretical lower bound of Theorem
2.1
, indicating that our estimate is minimax optimal when the sample size
n\gtrsim d^{2}
. In addition, in light of Theorem
2.4
, the sample complexity required by our estimate is also optimal, up to a logarithmic factor, among all estimates that can be computed using low-order polynomial algorithms.

3.4
Pre-whitening and Unknown Covariance Matrix

Throughout our discussion so far, we have made the simplifying assumption that the data are pre-whitened so that the mixing matrix
\mathbf{A}
is orthonormal. As noted before, this is always possible if the covariance matrix of
\mathbf{X}
is known apriori. In practice, however,
\texttt{cov}(\mathbf{X})
needs to be estimated. Prewhitening with an estimated covariance matrix could adversely affect the estimate for ICA. We shall now describe a sample splitting scheme to overcome this challenge so that all the properties we presented when
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
remain valid even if
\texttt{cov}(\mathbf{X})
is not known in advance.

Algorithm 3

Prewhitening

Input:

\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}

1:

Partition the samples into two halves with index sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.

2:

Compute the sample covariance matrix
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}
for
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}:i\in S_{1}\}
.

3:

Prewhiten data from
S_{2}
:
\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\leftarrow\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}_{i}
for
i\in S_{2}
.

4:

Run Algorithm
1
with input
\{\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{i}:i\in S_{2}\}
and initialized with Algorithm
2
. Denote by
\{\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}:1\leq j\leq d\}
the output.

5:

Update
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}\leftarrow\widehat{\Sigma}^{1/2}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}

6:

return

\{\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}:1\leq j\leq d\}
.

The validity of this algorithm is justified by the following result.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
s.t.
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2}>0
and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with bounded condition number. Suppose we run Algorithm
3
that calls Algorithm
1
and Algorithm
2
with parameters
L\geq C_{1}d^{2}
for initialization and
T\geq C_{2}\log d
for fixed-point iteration for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Denote by
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}=[\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{d}]
the output. There exist constants
C_{3},C_{4}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{3}d^{2}(\log\delta)^{2}
, then

\ell(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A})\leq C_{4}\sqrt{{d\log(1/\delta)}\over n},

with probability at least
1-\delta-d^{-3}-n^{-\epsilon/8}
with the loss function
\ell
being either
\ell_{M}
or
\ell_{A}
.

Theorem
3.4
shows that with the prewhitening via sample splitting as described by Algorithm
3
, we can ensure that the estimated mixing matrix enjoys the same asymptotic properties as if the true mixing matrix is orthonormal. In fact, in addition to the convergence rates, the asymptotic distributional properties we present in the next section continue to hold beyond orthonormal mixing matrices as well.

4
Asymptotic Normality

The computationally tractable estimators from the previous section are minimax rate optimal when
n\gtrsim d^{2}
. We now show that it is in fact possible to derive a finer bound, by an explicit characterization of the leading error term, enabling us to derive the asymptotic distribution of the estimators
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}
. For brevity, we shall assume, as before, that the mixing matrix
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
, and the estimated mixing directions are appropriately permuted and signed so that
\pi(j)=j
and
\langle\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{j}\rangle>0
. Specifically, this can be done by defining

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}:=\left[{\rm sign}(\langle\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{\pi(1)},\mathbf{a}_{1}\rangle)\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{\pi(1)}\,\,\dots\,\,{\rm sign}(\langle\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{\pi(d)},\mathbf{a}_{d}\rangle)\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{\pi(d)}\right]

(20)

where
\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}
s are the output from Algorithm
1
with initialization step given by Algorithm
2
. Specifically, we shall show that

{\rm sign}\left(\langle\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{j}\rangle\right)\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{\pi(j)}-\mathbf{a}_{j}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(S_{kj})^{3}\mathbf{X}_{k}+O_{p}\left(\dfrac{d(\log d)}{n}\right).

This allows us to derive asymptotic distributions for both linear and bilinear forms of
\mathbf{A}
.

Marginal Distribution of Linear Forms:

We begin with linear forms of unmixing directions. Consider, for example, estimating
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j}
for some fixed vector
\mathbf{u}
. A natural and consistent estimate is
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}
. The following theorem shows that it is also an asymptotically normal estimate.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
s.t.
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
,
M_{0},M_{2}>0
, and
\epsilon\geq 4
. Then for any
j\in[d]
, and any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\liminf_{d\to\infty}\|\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{a}_{j},\perp}\mathbf{u}\|>0
we have
for sufficiently large
d
that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert\mathbb{P}\left\{\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{\mathbf{u}}}\mathbf{u}^{\top}(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}-\mathbf{a}_{j})\leq x\right\}-\Phi(x)\right\rvert\leq~\dfrac{Cd(\log d)}{\sqrt{n}}+\dfrac{C(\log d)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{d}},

where
\sigma_{\mathbf{u}}^{2}=\mathbf{u}^{\top}(\mathbb{I}_{d}-\mathbf{a}_{j}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top})\mathbf{u}\cdot\Var(S_{1j}^{3})/\kappa_{4}(S_{j})^{2}
, provided
n\geq Cd^{2}(\log d)^{2}
.

It is clear from Theorem
4.1
that

\sqrt{n}\mathbf{u}^{\top}(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}-\mathbf{a}_{j})\to_{d}N(0,\sigma_{\mathbf{u}}^{2}),

when
d\to\infty
and
n\gg d^{2}(\log d)^{2}
. Note that, in Theorem
4.1
, we restrict ourselves to vector
\mathbf{u}
such that
\|\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{a}_{j},\perp}\mathbf{u}\|>0
for large
d
. This amounts to assuming that
\sin\angle\left(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{a}_{j}\right)>0
. On the other hand when
\sin\angle\left(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{a}_{j}\right)=0
, the estimated linear form converges at a faster rate and its asymptotic behavior can be more precisely characterized as follows:

Corollary 4.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
, we have for any
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
that

i)

for any
0<\delta<1
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left(1-\langle\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{j}\rangle^{2}\right)\geq\dfrac{Cd\log(1/\delta)}{n}+\dfrac{C(d\log d)^{3/2}}{n^{3/2}}\right)\leq\delta+d^{-3}+n^{-\epsilon/8}.

ii)

Moreover, if
n\geq Cd^{3}(\log d)^{2}
, then

\sup_{x>0}\,\left\lvert\mathbb{P}\left(\dfrac{n\kappa_{4}(S_{j})^{2}}{\mathbb{E}(S_{1j}^{6})^{2}}\left(1-\langle\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{j}\rangle^{2}\right)\leq x\right)-\mathbb{P}(\chi^{2}_{d}\leq x)\right\rvert\leq\dfrac{C(d\log d)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Note in particular that when
\|\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{a}_{j},\perp}\mathbf{u}\|\to 0
, then

\sqrt{n}\mathbf{u}^{\top}(\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}-\mathbf{a}_{j})\to_{p}0.

as
d,n\to\infty
while
n\gg d^{2}(\log d)^{2}
.

It is helpful to draw a comparison with the usual PCA, where an additional debiasing step may be required to obtain asymptotic normality for linear forms of the principal components. See, e.g.,
Koltchinskii and Lounici 2016
;
Koltchinskii and Lounici 2017
;
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
;
Xia 2021
. In the case of ICA, the sample size
n\gg d^{2}
required by computational consideration is sufficient to ensure that bias becomes negligible. A similar phenomenon was observed by
Xia et al. 2022
in the case of tensor denoising.

It is oftentimes of interest to derive the asymptotic distribution for individual entries of
\mathbf{A}
. This follows immediately from Theorem
4.1
by taking
\mathbf{u}
as the canonical basis.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
s.t.
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
,
M_{0},M_{2}>0
, and
\epsilon\geq 4
. If
|a_{ij}|=|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j}|<1-c
for some constant
c>0
, then under the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}~\left\lvert\mathbb{P}\left(\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{ij}}(\widehat{a}_{ij}-a_{ij})\leq x\right)-\Phi(x)\right\rvert\leq~\dfrac{Cd(\log d)}{\sqrt{n}}+\dfrac{C(\log d)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{d}}

where
\widehat{a}_{ij}
and
a_{ij}
are the
(i,j)
entry of
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathbf{A}
respectively, and

\sigma_{ij}^{2}=(1-a_{ij}^{2})\Var(S_{j}^{3})/\kappa_{4}(S_{j})^{2}
.

Joint Distribution of Linear Forms:

We can also derive the joint distribution of estimates of linear forms of multiple columns of
\mathbf{A}
. To this end, for
\mathbf{u}_{1},\mathbf{u}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denote by
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}}
a
d\times d
matrix whose
(i,j)
entry is given by

\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}}\right)_{ij}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{u}^{\top}_{j}(\mathbb{I}_{d}-\mathbf{a}_{j}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top})\mathbf{u}_{j}\cdot\ \dfrac{\Var(S_{j}^{3})}{\kappa_{4}(S_{j})^{2}}\quad&\text{if }i=j\\
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j}\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\ \dfrac{\mathbb{E}(S_{i}^{4})\mathbb{E}(S_{j}^{4})}{\kappa_{4}(S_{i})\kappa_{4}(S_{j})}\quad&\text{if }i\neq j.\end{cases}

Let us define the vectors
\mathbf{v},\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with elements

v_{j}:=\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{j}

and

\widehat{v}_{j}=\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}
. We then have

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
s.t.
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
,
M_{1},M_{2}>0
, and
\epsilon\geq 4
. Then for any
\mathbf{u}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\liminf_{d\to\infty}\min_{1\leq j\leq d}\|\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{a}_{j},\perp}\mathbf{u}_{j}\|>0
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{n}\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}}^{-1/2}(\widehat{\mathbf{v}}-\mathbf{v})\in\prod_{j=1}^{d}(-\infty,x_{j}]\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{d}\Phi(x_{j})\right\rvert\leq\dfrac{Cd(\log d)}{\sqrt{n}}+\dfrac{C(\log d)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{d}}.

for sufficiently large
d
, provided
n\geq Cd^{2}(\log d)^{2}
.

Bilinear Forms:

In addition to the linear forms described above, one may also be interested in bilinear forms
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}
for two vectors
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
. The asymptotic distribution of its estimate can also be derived.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
be
n
independent copies of
\mathbf{X}
s.t.
{\cal L}(\mathbf{X})\in{\cal P}_{\rm ICA}(\mathbf{A};\epsilon,M_{1},M_{2})
for some
\mathbf{A}\in{\cal O}(d)
,
M_{0},M_{2}>0
, and
\epsilon\geq 4
. Then for any
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\liminf_{d\to\infty}\sigma_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}~\left\lvert\mathbb{P}\left(\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}}\mathbf{u}^{\top}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{v}\leq x\right)-\Phi(x)\right\rvert\leq~\dfrac{Cd^{3/2}(\log d)}{\sqrt{n}}+\dfrac{C(\log d)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{d}},

for sufficiently large
d
, provided
n\geq Cd^{3}(\log d)^{2}
. Here
\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}:=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{u}

and

\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{v}}:={\rm diag}\left(\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq 1}\dfrac{v_{j}^{2}{\rm Var}(S_{1j}^{3})}{\kappa_{4}(S_{j})^{2}}\,\,\sum_{j\neq 2}\dfrac{v_{j}^{2}{\rm Var}(S_{1j}^{3})}{\kappa_{4}(S_{j})^{2}}\,\,\dots\,\,\sum_{j\neq d}\dfrac{v_{j}^{2}{\rm Var}(S_{1j}^{3})}{\kappa_{4}(S_{j})^{2}}\right).

Theorem
4.5
states the asymptotic normality of all bilinear forms
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}
for which the variance
\sigma_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
is positive. Notice that we require a larger sample size in Theorem
4.5
than for the linear forms. We suspect that the weaker bound in the previous theorem is an artifact of our proof and not a fundamental barrier.

5
Numerical Experiments

To complement our theoretical analysis and demonstrate the practical merits of our approach,
we carried out several sets of numerical experiments.

5.1
Finite Sample Properties

Our first set of numerical experiments is a number of simulation studies to explore the finite sample properties of the proposed method and its comparison with several popular ICA techniques.

Comparison with Other Popular Techniques.

In these experiments, we fixed the dimension
d=25
, generated the mixing matrix
\mathbf{A}
from the uniform distribution over
{\cal O}(d)
, and
S_{k}
s from the
{\rm Laplace}
distribution with mean zero and variance one. We varied the sample size
n=500
,
1500
, and
2000
. For each simulated data, we computed the mixing matrix using six different methods: FastICA using neg-entropy as the contrast function
(
Hyvärinen and Oja 2000
)
, JADE
(
Cardoso and Souloumiac 1993
;
Cardoso 1999
)
, FastICA initialized by naive matricization, FastICA with random initialization, FastICA with random slicing (
Anandkumar et al. 2014b
), and the proposed method. In each run, we measure the performance of both estimates using
\ell_{M}
and
\ell_{A}
. The two error measures yield qualitatively very similar results and we only report the result with
\ell_{A}
, for brevity. The results, summarized from 200 runs for each sample size, are presented in Figure
1
.

Comparison of ICA methods for
